Imploding ice shelves may have linked to ocean warming The disintegration of two immense Antarctic ice shelves last year -- and the 40 feet of ice loss that accompanied it -- might have been triggered by prolonged exposure to unusually warm ocean waters, scientists report here. But the likely culprit behind the "unprecedented" warming event was a freakish atmospheric instability, researchers said. In the last months of 2010, rivers of warm ocean water coursed up both sides of the of the two ice shelves -- Larsen A and Larsen B -- located about a thousand miles from the pole. The intrusion of warm, salty pack-ice-crushing water deep beneath the surface had melted nearly half the ice shelf floor. "What we see is two-thirds of an East Antarctic ice stream right now is actually unglaciated," said Adrian Jenkins, a glaciologist at Britain's Newcastle University and lead author of a study on the event, published Tuesday in the proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. Jenkins said the shelves' ice tongue, a horizontal extension that juts out into the sea, behaved as if it were floating in supercritical water.

Postpartum depression is typically considered a women's illness, but a recent study led by Virginia2 researchers says men can also get the blues after the birth of a child. "We always thought of postpartum depression as a woman's issue. That was just the assumption. No one that I'm aware of has actually studied postpartum depression in men," said Dr. James MacKay, lead author of the study and an associate professor of psychology at the University of Virginia.Researchers have a far better understanding of postpartum depression in women. For example, they know it can occur soon after childbirth, affects about 1 out of every 10 new mothers, and can last for several years if untreated. Yet, because information on PPD in men is scarce, doctors don't understand as well what causes it or how best to treat it, MacKay said. Q: US Growth Is Strong, but It Could Be Stronger WASHINGTON — For most of the better part of a decade, economic growth has never really been a problem in the United States. It has been tepid, but barely enough to create the conditions needed to push down the unemployment rate. American companies, flush with cash, are investing, while household incomes are rising slowly. But come January, in an election year, thanks to a quirk of the calendar, the United States hits a potential speed bump that threatens to slow growth significantly: There are simply a lot fewer working days between January and April. Even as American workers are kicking off 2016 with enthusiasm and confidence, engaging ever more in the labor force and taking on a growing number of jobs, they will encounter three fewer weeks of paid employment in the early months of the year than they did in 2015. “We actually lost 10 business days — two weeks — compared with all of last year,” said Francese Reisman, a senior economist for Oxford Economics. Pollutants from burning coal, called polychlorinated dibenzo-p-dioxins and dibenzofurans, commonly abbreviated as dioxins, have been known for more than 30 years to cause neurological problems in animals. Until now, these problems were assumed to be caused by the main component of dioxins, called 2,3,7,8-tetrachlorodibenzodioxin. However, a new study now shows that other components of dioxin, known as mono-ortho dioxins, may also cause nerve damage in rats based on the symptoms they display. Problem: Singapore offers records to pay debts Singapore is offering citizens the chance to pay back financial debts, taxes and student loans with personal information, such as details from income tax records. The Straits Times newspaper reports that this unusual method of debt collection has already been tried twice so far on indebted individuals, but the individual in question appealed to the courts on both occasions to avoid paying back their debts. Under a 2013 law, the Inland Revenue Authority of Singapore (IRAS) can seize information from the Central Provident Fund (CPF) of Singaporean citizens. In other words, no matter who you are connected to, whether it is a friend or family member, the IRAS will be able to get information about the assets and liabilities of the individual concerned. Further, employers and the CPF Boards of their respective employers have to hand this information over voluntarily or face criminal charges. About 82% of Singaporeans are members of CPF. Most companies and workplaces deduct CPF contributions from the salaries of their employees, which are then split between the employer and the employee. CPF

Here’s one: To make the Web clips and offline favorites that appear in the Chrome Web browser make more sense, Google changes their names to reflect the URL of the site they’re from. Unfortunately, Chrome periodically loses that information — prompting the frustrating problem of bookmarks that say, for example, “docs.google.com.” So, for the record, this is definitely a bug in Chrome — and one Google already knows about. “With newer builds of Chrome for Mac, the name of an offline page does indeed match the URL of that page,” Google explains on its issue tracker. “This change came in to make our local storage conform to common sniffing methods used by other browsers.” Although the logic behind the change makes sense, Chrome still hasn’t fixed it — nor does it offer any way to clear its database and restore historical error-prone names. Mrs Hawkes suffered 100 injuries, including a crushed hand, fractured ribs, a broken shoulder and a broken pelvis at a property where Mr Hurst ran an arm-wrestling event. The deadline for ISPs to comply with the California Online Privacy Protection Act is set to take effect on Monday. Early next year, California will become one of the only states with legal protections for individuals' browsing histories, search history, user names, and other data that many ISPs collect. Privacy advocates hope other states will start paying attention to state laws like this and join California in coming up with meaningful privacy regulations.
